\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle A_{y}^{k}\otimes B_{y}^{k}\rangle\right)+2d-3\geq 2d-2-\varepsilon

(4.100)

which on simplification yields us that

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi|A_{y}^{k}\otimes B_{y}^{k}|\psi\rangle\right|\geq\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle\psi|A_{y}^{k}\otimes B_{y}^{k}|\psi\rangle\right)\geq 1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(4.101)

Here, we also used the fact the absolute value of a complex number is always greater than or equal to its real value, that is,
|z|\geq
Re
(z)
for any complex number
z
.

Another observation that is important in our proof is that any bipartite state
{|{\psi}\rangle}
of local dimension
d
can be expressed in the computational basis as,

\displaystyle{|{\psi}\rangle}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}{|{i}\rangle}{|{b_{i}}\rangle},

(4.102)

where
\{{|{b_{i}}\rangle}\}
are set of
d
normalised vectors belonging to
\mathcal{H}_{B}
that might not be orthogonal. Also
\alpha_{i}
are real numbers greater than or equal to zero that satisfy the normalisation condition
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
.

The proof for bounding the distance between the actual state
{|{\psi}\rangle}
and the ideal state
{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}
is divided into two major parts. We first show that
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}s
are close to
1/\sqrt{d}
. Then, we show that the real part of the terms
\langle i|b_{i}\rangle
are close to 1. The proof for finding robustness of the measurements (
4.98
) and (
4.99
) is quite straightforward and follows directly from the robustness of state (
4.97
) using simple manipulations.

Let us first consider the inequality (
4.101
) for
y=1
, and then substitute into it
A_{1}=X_{d}Z_{d}^{l}
and the state (
4.102
), which gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\left(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}{\langle{j}|}\!{\langle{b_{j}}|}\left[\left(X_{d}Z_{d}^{l}\right)^{k}\otimes B_{1}^{k}\right]\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}{|{i}\rangle}{|{b_{i}}\rangle}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon

(4.103)

for every
k
. Notice that

(X_{d}Z_{d}^{l})^{k}{|{i}\rangle}=\omega^{kl\left(i+\frac{k-1}{2}\right)}{|{i+k}\rangle}
, where the sum
i+k
is modulo
d
. Thus, we have that

\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+k}\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{kl\left(i+\frac{k-1}{2}\right)}\langle b_{i+k}|B_{1}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(4.104)

Using the fact that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{kl\left(i+\frac{k-1}{2}\right)}\langle b_{i+k}|B_{1}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle\right)\leq\left|\omega^{kl\left(i+\frac{k-1}{2}\right)}\langle b_{i+k}|B_{1}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle\right|\leq\left|\langle b_{i+k}|B_{1}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle\right|\leq 1,

(4.105)

where we used that
{|{b_{i}}\rangle}
are normalised, we have finally have that

\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+k}\geq 1-\varepsilon\quad\forall k.

(4.106)

Notice that the above relation holds trivially for
k=0
from the normalisation condition. Summing the above relation over
k
, we obtain

\sum_{i,k=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+k}=\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\right)^{2}\geq d(1-\varepsilon),

(4.107)

which gives

\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\geq\sqrt{d}\sqrt{1-\varepsilon}.

(4.108)

Let us now consider the following expression,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\left(\alpha_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}+1

(4.109)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\right),

where we used the normalisation condition
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
. The right hand side of the above equation can be upper bounded using (
4.108
) and thus we can finally conclude that
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}s
are close to
1/\sqrt{d}
by a factor of
\sqrt{2\varepsilon}
, that is,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\left(\alpha_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(1-\sqrt{1-\varepsilon})

(4.110)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon,

where we used the fact that
\sqrt{1-\varepsilon}\geq 1-\varepsilon
for any
0\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
and thus,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}-\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\leq\alpha_{i}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

(4.111)

Considering the expression
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\left(\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{2}
for any
i,j=0,\ldots,d-1
, in a similar manner as done above we can conclude that

\frac{1}{d}-\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\leq\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+j}\leq\frac{1}{d}+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

(4.112)

We now consider the condition (
4.101
) for
y=2
by substituting
A_{2}=Z_{d}
and the state (
4.102
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\left(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}{\langle{j}|}\!{\langle{b_{j}}|}\left[Z_{d}^{k}\otimes B_{2}^{k}\right]\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}{|{i}\rangle}{|{b_{i}}\rangle}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon\quad\forall k.

(4.113)

Notice that
Z_{d}^{k}{|{i}\rangle}=\omega^{ik}{|{i}\rangle}
and thus the above equation simplifies to

\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}\,\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{ik}\langle b_{i}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(4.114)

Again, using the property that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}(\omega^{ik}\langle b_{i}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle)\leq|\omega^{ik}\langle b_{i}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle|\leq|\langle b_{i}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{i}\rangle|\leq 1

(4.115)

for every
i,k
as
{|{b_{i}}\rangle}
is normalised. Thus, we can choose an index
j
such that

\sum_{i\neq j}\alpha_{i}^{2}+\alpha_{j}^{2}\,\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{jk}\langle b_{j}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

(4.116)

Using the normalisation condition
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
, we arrive at

\alpha_{j}^{2}\left[1-\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{jk}\langle b_{j}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\right]\leq\varepsilon.

(4.117)

Again using the property (
4.112
),and then the inequality (
4.111
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\left[1-\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{jk}\langle b_{j}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon+\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\left[1-\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{jk}\langle b_{j}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\right]

(4.118)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon+2\sqrt{2\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 3\sqrt{2\varepsilon},

where we used the fact that the maximum of the term
\left[1-\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{jk}\langle b_{j}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\right]
is two. Thus, we can finally conclude that

\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{jk}\langle b_{j}|B_{2}^{k}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\geq 1-3d\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

(4.119)

Recall that the eigenvalues of
B_{2}
is
\{1,\omega,\ldots,\omega^{d-1}\}
and thus we can consider the eigendecomposition of
B_{2}
using orthogonal projections
P_{i}
as

B_{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\omega^{i}P_{i}.

(4.120)

Here
P_{i}
in general might be of rank higher than one.
Plugging this decomposition into the condition (
4.119
), we have that

\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\mathrm{Re}\left(\omega^{(i+j)k}\langle b_{j}|P_{i}|b_{j}\rangle\right)\geq 1-3d\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\quad\forall k.

(4.121)

Notice that the above equation holds trivially for
k=0
. Taking a sum over
k
gives us

\langle b_{j}|P_{-j}|b_{j}\rangle\geq 1-3d\sqrt{2\varepsilon},

(4.122)

which implies that each vector
{|{b_{j}}\rangle}
is close to the projection
P_{-j}
which signifies the subspace corresponding to the
(d-j)-th
outcome of
B_{2}
. Now, let us look at the following normalised vectors

\displaystyle{|{\overline{v}_{j}}\rangle}=\frac{P_{-j}{|{b_{j}}\rangle}}{\|P_{-j}{|{b_{j}}\rangle}\|},

(4.123)

where we can infer that
{|{\overline{v}_{j}}\rangle}
are mutually orthogonal as well, that is,
{\langle{\overline{v}_{i}}|}\overline{v}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
. Note that, from the condition (
4.122
) the normalisation factor of each of the vector
{|{\overline{v}_{j}}\rangle}
is close to one, that is,
\|P_{-j}{|{b_{j}}\rangle}\|\geq 1-2d\sqrt{2\varepsilon}
. Further, using these vectors we can express
B_{2}
as

B_{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\omega^{-i}{|{\overline{v}_{i}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\overline{v}_{i}}|}\oplus B_{2}^{\prime},

(4.124)

such that
B_{2}^{\prime}
is some operator acting on the support orthogonal to the subspace spanned by the vectors
\{{|{\overline{v}_{i}}\rangle}\}
. Now, there always exist a unitary
U_{B}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
U_{B}{|{\overline{v}_{i}}\rangle}={|{i}\rangle}
and thus,

U_{B}B_{2}U_{B}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\omega^{-i}{|{i}\rangle}\!{\langle{i}|}\oplus B_{2}^{\prime\prime}.

(4.125)

Substituting the above form of
B_{2}
to (
4.119
) and then taking a sum over
k
, we can also deduce that

\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle b_{i}|U_{B}^{\dagger}|i\rangle\right)\geq 1-3d\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

(4.126)

Finally, we can compute the distance between the states (
4.97
) using the above results as,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes U_{B}{|{{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi^{+}_{d}}\rangle}\right\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{2\left[1-\mathrm{Re}(\langle{\psi}|U_{B}^{\dagger}|\phi_{d}^{+}\rangle)\right]\right\}^{1/2}

(4.127)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{2\left[1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\,\mathrm{Re}(\langle b_{i}|U_{B}^{\dagger}|i\rangle)\right]\right\}^{1/2}.

Now, using the conditions (
4.108
) and (
4.126
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\,\mathrm{Re}(\langle b_{i}|U_{B}^{\dagger}|i\rangle)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-3d\sqrt{2\varepsilon})\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}

(4.128)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-3d\sqrt{2\varepsilon})\sqrt{d}\sqrt{1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sqrt{d}\left[1-(3d+1)\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\right],

where the inequality in the third line is a consequence of two inequalities, first,
\sqrt{1-\varepsilon}\geq 1-\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon\leq 1
and second,
\varepsilon\leq\sqrt{2\varepsilon}
. Using the inequality (
4.128
), we finally obtain the robustness of the state (
4.97
) as

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes U_{B}\right){|{{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi^{+}_{d}}\rangle}\right\|\leq\sqrt{2(3d+1)}\sqrt[4]{2\varepsilon}.

(4.129)

Let us now prove the inequalities (
4.98
) and (
4.99
). For simplicity, we denote the ideal observables as
B_{i}^{\prime}
and
U_{B}B_{i}U_{B}^{\dagger}=\widetilde{B}_{i}
. We first observe that the Hilbert Schmidt norm can be written as a vector norm using
{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}
as

\left\|\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}\right\|_{2}=\sqrt{d}\left\|\left[\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}\right]{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}\right\|.

(4.130)

Then, using the triangle inequality
|A+B|\geq\left||A|-|B|\right|
and denoting
{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}=\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes U_{B}{|{\psi}\rangle}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}\right\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}+(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}-\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}\right\|

(4.131)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}\right]{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}\right\|-\left\|\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}({|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle})\right\|,

Then using using the inequality (
4.130
) and the fact that
B_{i}
is unitary, leads us to

\left\|\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\left(\left\|\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-(B^{\prime}_{i})^{k}{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}\right\|+\left\|{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi_{d}^{+}}\rangle}\right\|\right).

(4.132)

The first term in the right hand side of the above inequality can be computed using (
4.127
) by first noting that
A_{i}^{k}\otimes B_{i}^{\prime k}{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}={|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}
and then using the fact that
A_{i}
is unitary,

\displaystyle\left\|(A_{i}^{k}\otimes\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}){|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}+{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi^{+}_{d}}\rangle}\right\|\leq\left\|(A_{i}^{k}\otimes\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}){|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}\right\|+\left\|{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi^{+}_{d}}\rangle}\right\|.

(4.133)

Finally using the conditions (
4.101
) we get

\displaystyle\left\|(A_{i}^{k}\otimes\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}){|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}\right\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|(A_{i}^{k}\otimes B_{i}^{k}){|{\psi}\rangle}-{|{\psi}\rangle}\right\|

(4.134)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{2\left[1-\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle\psi|A_{i}^{k}\otimes B_{i}^{k}|\psi\rangle\right)\right]\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\varepsilon}.

Using the above inequality, we get that

\displaystyle\left\|(A_{i}^{k}\otimes\widetilde{B}_{i}^{k}){|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}+{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi^{+}_{d}}\rangle}\right\|\leq\sqrt{2\varepsilon}+2\left\|{|{\widetilde{\psi}}\rangle}-{|{\phi^{+}_{d}}\rangle}\right\|,

(4.135)

and then using the condition (
4.97
) we obtain
(
4.98
) and (
4.99
).
∎

4.5
Conclusions and discussions

We proposed a one-sided device-independent scheme for a certification of a large family of incompatible measurements with arbitrary number of outcomes termed here genuinely incompatible. Our scheme also allows for the certification of the two-qudit maximally entangled of any arbitrary local dimension using only two measurements on each side. This is the first certification of mutually unbiased bases of any dimension using quantum steering. Unlike the previous approaches in literature, our scheme is more general in the sense that we do not assume that the state shared between the parties is pure or the measurements on the untrusted side are projective. This makes our scheme even significant in cryptographic scenarios where there can be an external Eve who can have access to the state as well as the untrusted measurements. We also find a simple technique for robust certification of a smaller family of genuinely incompatible observables including the complete mutually unbiased bases of any prime dimension and pair of them for non-prime dimensions. We also considered the scenario when the measurements are not genuinely incompatible and observed that only a part of the measurement can be certified based on the quantum state that realises the quantum bound of the steering functional.

One of the drawbacks of the 1SDI scheme as compared to fully device-independent schemes is that one has to assume that one of the parties is trusted. However, they still posses some of the essential features that would be useful in certification of quantum technologies. First, if one possesses a well characterised quantum device, here it is the measuring device of the trusted party, our scheme provides a way to compare any other untrusted device with the trusted device using minimal resources. In other words, our scheme given a trusted measurement device allows one to verify that any other device performs the desired measurements. Second,
our scheme is applicable to every 1SDI scenario where a client wants to verify the state supplied by an untrusted source along with the untrusted measuring device. Thus, one-sided quantum key distribution schemes
[
121
,
122
]
and randomness generation would be two major applications of our certification scheme. Third, even from a practical point of view, implementing 1SDI protocols is much easier than the DI protocols
[
121
]
. The reason being that demonstration of quantum steering is practically more robust to noise and can be observed with detectors of lower efficiencies than Bell violations
[
174
,
175
]
. Fourth, there are a very few analytical methods dedicated to the field of quantum certification, let alone to higher dimensional quantum certification. Thus, purely from a mathematical perspective, we introduced techniques that can be relevant for other schemes that aim to characterise arbitrary dimensional quantum systems. Finally, in certain scenarios 1SDI schemes can be made device-independent if one can device-independently characterise the measuring device of the trusted party as pointed out recently in Ref.
[
3
]
.

Some interesting follow-up problems arise from our work. The most important among them would be whether our 1SDI scheme can be made fully device-independent, that is, the question whether it is possible to design a scheme for certifying every set of genuinely incompatible observables in a device-independent way still remains open. Another interesting problem would be to find 1SDI scheme that can be used for certification of incompatible observables that are not genuine incompatible. It would also be interesting to construct steering functionals whose quantum bound is saturated by non-maximally entangled state of arbitrary local dimension
d
and thus eventually finding a scheme allowing for certification of every bipartite entangled state. This problem is tackled in the next chapter of this thesis. An ambitious problem would be extend the idea of genuine incompatibility to non-projective schemes and then find steering functionals for certification of POVM’s.

Chapter 5
Certification of any pure bipartite entangled state and optimal randomness using quantum steering

5.1
Introduction

Generating genuine random outputs inaccessible to hackers is one of the key steps in any key distribution protocol, be it classical or quantum. As discussed before in Chapter
2
, in classical physics the security of such protocols relies on the fact that large numbers can not be efficiently factorized using classical computers. However, using quantum computers such protocols can be broken in polynomial time. As was shown in
[
76
]
, violation of Bell inequalities serve as the most secure way to certify genuine randomness. However,
from a practical point of view, performing a Bell experiment is extremely challenging as one requires very low levels of noise along with the detectors being highly efficient. As a consequence, we need to consider scenarios where one can efficiently generate secure randomness, easier to implement, require minimal resources and are robust to noise. We showed in Chapter
3
that one can securely generate
\log_{2}d
bits of randomness using a quantum system of dimension
d
. From a foundational point of view, it still remains an open and highly non-trivial problem whether one can securely generate the optimal amount of randomness using a quantum system of arbitrary dimension
d
which is
2\log_{2}d
bits.

In this chapter, we aim to solve the above problem by considering the one-sided device-independent (1SDI) scenario where the required resource is quantum steering. As a matter of fact, it was shown in
[
121
]
that quantum steering can be observed using detectors with much lower efficiency and more noise-robust when compared to observing Bell non-locality. This makes 1SDI setting an ideal scenario to construct protocols for randomness generation that can be practically implemented. Randomness generation in 1SDI setting has been recently explored in
[
132
,
176
]
. Particularly in Ref.
[
176
]
, the authors construct a protocol for generating
\log_{2}d
bits of randomness from a quantum system of dimension
d
in a secure way by using
d
-outcome projective measurements.

To this end, we first construct a family of steering inequalities maximally violated by any pure entangled state of local dimension
d
and two
d-
outcome measurements on each side. Using the maximal violation of the steering inequalities, we then certify any pure bipartite entangled state and a pair of mutually unbiased bases of arbitrary dimension on the untrusted side. This is the first instance, where any pure bipartite entangled state can be certified using the least number of measurements required to observe quantum non-locality, that is, using only two measurements on each side. Additionally, we demonstrate that any rank-one extremal measurement can be certified using our protocol. Based on these results, we finally demonstrate the certification of
2\log_{2}d
bits of randomness using the certified entangled state of local dimension
d
and the certified
d^{2}-
outcome extremal measurement. We further show that for systems of dimension
d=3,4,5,6
, the optimal amount of randomness can be certified using partially entangled states. This further strengthens our scheme as one can generate highest amount of randomness by employing less resource in terms of entanglement.

5.2
Family of steering inequalities

Let us shortly describe again the quantum steering scenario introduced in Chapter
2
and also in Chapter
4
in an analogous way to Bell scenario. Alice and Bob are located in spatially separated labs. Both of them receive two subsystems from a preparation device. Alice is trusted and performs two known
d-
outcome measurements on the received subsystem labelled by
x=1,2
. In our case, we consider these measurements in the observable form as
A_{0}=Z_{d}
and
A_{1}=X_{d}
which as described in Eq. (
2.91
) of Chapter
4
, constitute a pair of mutually unbiased bases. Bob also performs two
d-
outcome measurements on his subsystem labelled by
y=1,2
. They collect enough statistics to construct the joint probability distribution
\{p(a,b|x,y)\}
. The scenario is depicted in Fig.
4.1
of Chapter
4
such that
N=2
.

We now construct a family of steering inequalities which is expressed in the observable picture and using a collection of positive non-zero numbers
\bm{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d-1}\}
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
, as

I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left\langle A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})A_{1}^{k}\otimes B_{k|1}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})A_{0}^{k}\right\rangle\leq\beta_{L}(\bm{\alpha}),

(5.1)

where the coefficients
\gamma(\bm{\alpha})
and
\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})
are given by

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})=d\left({\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=0\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}}\right)^{-1},\qquad\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})=-\frac{\gamma(\bm{\alpha})}{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=0\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}\omega^{k(d-j)}.

(5.2)

Notice that
\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\geq 0
for any choice of
\bm{\alpha}
and
\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})
are in general complex. Alice is trusted (or fully characterised), and her measurements are expressed in the observable picture as

A_{0}=Z_{d}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\omega^{i}{|{i}\rangle}\!{\langle{i}|},\qquad A_{1}=X_{d}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}{|{i+1}\rangle}\!{\langle{i}|}.

(5.3)

Recall that the set of vectors
\{{|{i}\rangle}\}_{i=0}^{d-1}
represents the computational basis in
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
. It is worth noting here that the expression
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
(
2.67
) is real for any choice of
\bm{\alpha}
. This is because
B_{d-k|i}=(B_{k|i})^{\dagger}
for any generalised observable (see Eq. (
2.22
) of Chapter
2
) and also that the coefficient
\delta_{d-k}(\bm{\alpha})=\delta_{k}^{*}(\bm{\alpha})
for any
k
.
Let us now express the steering functional in (
2.67
) using the joint probability picture as

\displaystyle I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=d\sum_{a,b=0}^{d-1}c_{a,b}p(a,b|0,0)\!

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\!\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(d\sum_{a,b=0}^{d-1}c_{a,b}p(a,b|1,1)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,a=0\\
i\neq a\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\frac{p(a|0)}{\alpha_{a}}\right)

(5.4)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\!1-\gamma(\bm{\alpha})-\delta_{0}(\bm{\alpha}),

such that

\displaystyle c(a,b)=\begin{cases}1\quad\text{if}\quad a\oplus_{d}b=0\\
0\quad\text{otherwise}\ \ \ \ \ \ ,\end{cases}

(5.5)

where
a\oplus_{d}b
represents
a+b
modulo
d
. To arrive at the above expression we used the fourier transform to express the expectation values in terms of the joint probabilities as defined in (
4.4
).
Note from Eq. (
5.2
) that
\delta_{0}(\bm{\alpha})=-1
, which implies that

\displaystyle I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=d\sum_{a,b=0}^{d-1}c_{a,b}p(a,b|0,0)+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(d\sum_{a,b=0}^{d-1}c_{a,b}p(a,b|1,1)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}\frac{p(a|0)}{\alpha_{a}}\right).

Using the above expression of the steering functional, let us now compute the classical bound
\beta_{L}(\bm{\alpha})
of the above steering inequality.

5.2.1
Classical bound

As derived before in Eq. (
4.7
) of Chapter
4
, to find the classical bound of a steering functional, we need to consider an LHS model such that the joint probability distribution can be expressed as

p(a,b|x,y)=\sum_{\lambda}p(\lambda)p(a|x,\rho_{\lambda})p(b|y,\lambda),

(5.7)

where
\lambda
denotes some unknown variables the collection of which is denoted by
\lambda
. These variables occur with a probability distribution
p(\lambda)
. Here
p(a|x,\rho_{\lambda})
is the local probability of Alice obtaining an outcome
a
given the input
x
and some quantum state
\rho_{\lambda}
acts on
\mathbbm{C}^{d}

1
1

1

This is due to the fact that Alice is trusted and is known to perform quantum measurments on some quantum state.
and
p(b|y,\lambda)
is the local probability distribution depending on some unknown variable
\lambda
.
For such a probability distribution (
5.7
), the steering functional from Eq. (
5.2
) can be expressed as,

\displaystyle I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\sum^{d-1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}a=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\lambda}\ p(\lambda)p(a|0,\rho_{\lambda})p(d-a|0,\lambda)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(d\sum^{d-1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}a=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\lambda}\ p(\lambda)p(a|1,\rho_{\lambda})p(d-a|1,\lambda)-\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}\sum_{\lambda}\frac{p(\lambda)p(a|0,\rho_{\lambda})}{\alpha_{a}}\right),

where to obtain the last term, we used the no-signalling conditions given in Eq. (
2.53
) of Chapter
2
such that

\displaystyle p(a|0)=\sum_{b}p(a,b|0,y)\qquad\forall y.

(5.9)

Notice that in the above expression we used the fact that
\sum_{b}p(b|y,\lambda)=1
for any
\lambda
and then denoted
\rho_{A}=\sum_{\lambda}p(\lambda)\rho_{\lambda}
.
Let us first consider the first two terms in Eq. (
5.2.1
) and find their upper bound in the following way,

\displaystyle I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d\sum_{\lambda}\ p(\lambda)\max_{a}p(a|0,\rho_{\lambda})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(d\sum_{\lambda}\ p(\lambda)\max_{a}p(a|1,\rho_{\lambda})-\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}\sum_{\lambda}\frac{p(\lambda)p(a|0,\rho_{\lambda})}{\alpha_{a}}\right),

where to obtain the first inequality we used the fact that
\sum_{a}p(d-a|y,\lambda)=1
for any
y
and
\lambda
and then used the mathematical identity
\sum_{i}s_{i}w_{i}\leq\max_{i}\{s_{i}\}
whenever
w_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}w_{i}=1
.
Now as was done before in Chapter
4
[see Eq. (
4.12
)] and using the fact that
\sum_{\lambda}p(\lambda)=1
, we get that

I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})\leq\max_{\rho}\left[d\max_{a}\{p(a|0,\rho)\}+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(d\max_{a}\{p(a|1,\rho)\}-\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}\frac{p(a|0,\rho)}{\alpha_{a}}\right)\right].

(5.11)

Notice the above expression is convex in the state
\rho
. As a consequence, the maximisation in the above expression can be taken over pure states
{|{\psi}\rangle}\in\mathbbm{C}^{d}
.
Now expressing the state
{|{\psi}\rangle}
in the computational basis of
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
as

{|{\psi}\rangle}=\sum_{i}\eta_{i}{|{i}\rangle}
,
and then plugging in Alice’s observables,
A_{0}=Z_{d}

and
A_{1}=X_{d}
, the formula (
5.11
) can be rewritten as

I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\eta_{0}|,\ldots,|\eta_{d-1}|\\
|\eta_{0}|^{2}+\ldots+|\eta_{d-1}|^{2}=1\end{subarray}}\left\{d\max_{a}\{|\eta_{a}|^{2}\}+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left[\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}|\eta_{i}|\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}\frac{|\eta_{a}|^{2}}{\alpha_{a}}\right]\right\}.

(5.12)

Given any arbitrary collection of positive numbers
\bm{\alpha}
such that the sum of the squares of those numbers is one, it is not straightforward to find this bound. However, we can show here that the right hand side of the above formula
is strictly less than
d
for any
\bm{\alpha}
. We prove this claim using the technique of contradiction. Let us first consider the term inside the square brackets of the above expression (
5.12
) and show that it is always negative. For this, let us recall the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for positive real numbers also known as Sedrakyan’s inequality
[
177
]
,

\displaystyle\frac{(\sum_{i}u_{i})^{2}}{\sum_{i}v_{i}}\leq\sum_{i}\frac{u_{i}^{2}}{v_{i}}.

(5.13)

Now, substituting
u_{i}=|\eta_{i}|
and
v_{i}=\alpha_{i}
, we can rewrite the above expression as

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}|\eta_{i}|\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\frac{|\eta_{i}|^{2}}{\alpha_{i}}.

(5.14)

Thus, we can conclude from (
5.12
) that the the classical bound of
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
is less than or equal to
d
, that is,

\displaystyle I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})\leq d\max_{a}\{|\eta_{a}|^{2}\}\leq d.

(5.15)

We now show that the above inequlity can not be saturated by L.H.S. models. To this end, let us assume that
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=d
. This implies that the term inside the square brackets in (
5.12
) vanishes and thus,

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}|\eta_{i}|\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\frac{|\eta_{i}|^{2}}{\alpha_{i}}.

(5.16)

along with the first term

\max_{a}\{|\eta_{a}|^{2}\}=1.

(5.17)

Recall that equality holds in the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (
5.13
) iff
u_{i}=\kappa v_{i}
for all
i
where
\kappa
is some real coefficient. Thus, in (
5.16
)
\alpha_{i}=\kappa|\eta_{i}|
for each
i
. Using the condition
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i}|\eta_{i}|^{2}=1
and that
\alpha_{i}>0
for any
i
, imposes that
\kappa=1
, and therefore
\alpha_{i}=|\eta_{i}|
. But
\alpha_{i}<1
for any
i
which contradicts the second condition (
5.17
). Thus, our initial assumption is wrong which implies that the maximal classical value of the steering function
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
is strictly less than
d
for arbitrary collection of positive numbers
\alpha_{i}
such that
\alpha_{0}^{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{d-1}^{2}=1
. Now, we move onto finding the quantum bound of the steering functional in (
2.67
).

5.2.2
Quantum bound

Here we show that the maximum value of the steering functional
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
in Eq. (
5.1
) obtainable using quantum states and measurements is given by
\beta_{Q}(\bm{\alpha})=d
. The result is stated below as a mathematical theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

For any collection of positive real numbers
\bm{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d-1}\}
such that
\alpha_{0}^{2}+\ldots+\alpha_{d-1}^{2}=1
,
the quantum bound of the steering functional
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
is independent of
\bm{\alpha}
and is given by

\beta_{Q}(\bm{\alpha})=d
.

Proof.

Let us begin by introducing the steering operator corresponding to
the steering functional
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
in (
5.1
),

\hat{I}_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})A_{1}^{k}\otimes B_{k|1}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})A_{0}^{k}\right).

(5.18)

Recall that
A_{0}=Z_{d}
and
A_{1}=X_{d}
and
B_{i}^{\prime}s
are any
d
-outcome generalised observables corresponding to the measurements of Bob. Our aim is to show that

\beta_{Q}(\bm{\alpha})=\max_{\rho_{AB},B_{i}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{I}_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})\rho_{AB}\right]=d,

(5.19)

where
\rho_{AB}
acting on
\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
represents some quantum state shared between Alice and Bob and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
represents the Hilbert space of Bob of arbitrary but finite dimension. As the expression (
5.19
) is linear, we can optimise this over pure states
{|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle}\in\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
, that is,

\max_{{|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle},B_{i}}\langle\psi_{AB}|\hat{I}_{2,d,2}|\psi_{AB}\rangle=d.

(5.20)

For simplicity, in the rest of the proof we drop the subscript
AB
from the state. We first show that the expectation value of the steering operator for any
{|{\psi}\rangle}
is upper bounded by
d
and then find a quantum realisation that achieves this bound. For this purpose, let us break the steering operator
\hat{I}_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})
into two parts as

\hat{I}_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}+S(\bm{\alpha})

(5.21)

such that

S(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left[\gamma(\bm{\alpha})A_{1}^{k}\otimes B_{k|1}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})A_{0}^{k}\right].

(5.22)

Notice that the above operator is hermitian which is due to the fact that
A_{0}^{d-k}=(A_{0}^{k})^{\dagger}
and
B_{d-k|0}=B_{k|0}^{\dagger}
[see Eq. (
2.22
)]. It is trivial to see that the absolute values of the expectation value of each term in the first part of operator in Eq. (
5.21
) is less than or equal to
1
as
A_{0}
is unitary and
B_{k|0}^{\dagger}B_{k|0}\leq\mathbb{1}
for any
k
[see Eq. (
2.23
)], that is,

|\langle\psi|A^{k}_{0}\otimes B_{k|0}|\psi\rangle|\leq 1,

(5.23)

for any
{|{\psi}\rangle}
and
k=1,\ldots,d-1
.
This allows us to conclude from (
5.21
) that

\displaystyle\langle\psi|\hat{I}_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d-1+\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle.

(5.24)

Now, we demonstrate for any
{|{\psi}\rangle}
that
\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle\leq 1
. For this purpose, let us first notice that the state
{|{\psi}\rangle}
belongs to
\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. Thus, as discussed before in Chapter
2
any such state can be written using the computational basis in
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
as

{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}{|{i}\rangle}_{A}{|{e_{i}}\rangle}_{B},

(5.25)

where
\lambda_{i}
are real and non-negative numbers such that
\lambda_{0}^{2}+\ldots+\lambda_{d-1}^{2}=1
, and
{|{e_{i}}\rangle}
are vectors belonging to
\mathcal{H}_{B}
which are not orthogonal in general. Plugging in this state, we find the expectation value of
S(\bm{\alpha})
as

\displaystyle\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\left[\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}{\langle{i}|}A_{1}^{k}{|{j}\rangle}{\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{j}}\rangle}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}{\langle{i}|}A_{0}^{k}{|{j}\rangle}\!{\langle{e_{i}}|}e_{j}\rangle\right]

(5.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\left[\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-k}{\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}^{2}\omega^{ik}\right],

where to arrive at the second equality, we plugged in the explicit forms of
A_{i}^{\prime}s
and then used the fact that
Z_{d}^{k}{|{i}\rangle}=\omega^{ki}{|{i}\rangle}
and
X_{d}^{k}{|{i}\rangle}={|{i+k}\rangle}
.
Focusing on the second term of the above expression and plugging in

\delta_{0}(\bm{\alpha})=-1
, we obtain that

\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}^{2}\omega^{ik}=1+\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}^{2}\omega^{ik}.

(5.27)

Using then the explicit form of
\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})
given in Eq. (
5.2
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})\lambda_{i}^{2}\omega^{ik}=1+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})-\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}\lambda_{j}^{2},

(5.28)

where we also used the identity

\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\omega^{k(i-j)}=d\delta_{ij}.

(5.29)

Notice that since
\gamma(\bm{\alpha})
is real, the above expression (
5.28
) is also real.
Since,
S(\bm{\alpha})
is a Hermitian operator we have that any expectation value of this operator is real.
Plugging the relations (
5.28
) and remembering that
\lambda_{i}^{\prime}s
are also real, we can rewrite Eq. (
5.26
) as

\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle=1+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-k}\mathrm{Re}\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)-\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}\lambda_{j}^{2}.

(5.30)

Exploiting the fact that
\mathrm{Re}(z)\leq|z|
for any
z\in\mathbbm{C}
and that
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\leq\mathbb{1}
for any
k
, we get that
\mathrm{Re}\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)\leq 1
, Thus, we finally arrive at

\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle\leq 1+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left[\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\alpha_{j}}\right].

(5.31)

Now, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
5.13
) in which we substitute
u_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
v_{i}=\alpha_{i}
, we can conclude that the term inside the square brackets of the above expression is less than or equal to
0
.
Thus, we can finally conclude that the expectation value of
S(\bm{\alpha})
for any state
{|{\psi}\rangle}
is less than or equal to
1
. that is,

\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle\leq 1

(5.32)

and hence, putting it back into (
5.24
), we obtain

\langle\psi|\hat{I}_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle\leq d,

(5.33)

for any
{|{\psi}\rangle}\in\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. It is also interesting to note here that the relation (
5.32
) is saturated when

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}}{\alpha_{j}}.

(5.34)

Now, using the fact that Sedrakyan’s inequality (
5.13
) is saturated if
u_{i}=\kappa v_{i}
for some
\kappa\in\mathbbm{C}
. Substituting
u_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
v_{i}=\alpha_{i}
in the Sedrakyan’s inequality (
5.13
), we see that
\lambda_{i}=\kappa\alpha_{i}
for all
i
. Now, using normalisation we get that the only solution of Eq. (
5.34
) is
\lambda_{i}=e^{i\phi}\alpha_{i}
for some arbitrary phase
\phi
. As
\alpha_{i}>0
, we get that
|\lambda_{i}|>0
for all
i
. Thus, we can simply conclude that any state saturating the inequality (
5.32
) is locally full-rank.

Let us now consider a family of states parametrized by the collection of positive real coefficients
\bm{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}_{AB}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}{|{i}\rangle}_{A}{|{i}\rangle}_{B},

(5.35)

where the local bases of (
5.35
) is the computational basis of
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
. Notice that the above state is a valid normalised quantum state
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
. Notice also that every pure bipartite entangled state of Schmidt rank
d
can be expressed as these states (
5.35
) up to local unitary transformations. Now, consider Bob’s observables to be projective and to satisfy

\displaystyle B_{0}=Z_{d}^{*},\qquad B_{1}=X_{d}.

(5.36)

Plugging this state and the observables in the steering functional in (
5.1
), we get that
I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=d
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Notice that the maximal violation of the steering inequality
(
5.1
) by a state
{|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle}
and Bob’s observables

B_{i}
can only be achieved iff the inequalities (
5.40
) and (
5.43
) are saturated. Thus, we arrive at the following conditions

\langle\psi|A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}|\psi\rangle=1

(5.37)

for any
k=1,\ldots,d-1
as well as

\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle=1,

(5.38)

where
S(\bm{\alpha})
is defined in Eq. (
5.22
). Now, consider the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for vectors given as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}(\langle u|v\rangle)\leq|\langle u|v\rangle|\leq\left|\langle{u}{|{u}\rangle}\langle{v}{|{v}\rangle}\right|.

(5.39)

From the condition (
5.37
), let us now substitute
{|{u}\rangle}={|{\psi}\rangle}
and
{|{v}\rangle}=A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}|\psi\rangle
in the above inequality (
5.39
). Then using the fact that
B_{k|0}^{\dagger}B_{k|0}\leq\mathbb{1}
, we get that both the L.H.S. and R.H.S. of (
5.13
) are equal to one. This can only happen iff
{|{u}\rangle}
and
{|{v}\rangle}
are linearly dependent, that is,
{|{u}\rangle}=\lambda{|{v}\rangle}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbbm{C}
. As both
{|{u}\rangle}
and
{|{v}\rangle}
are normalised, we get that
{|{u}\rangle}=e^{i\phi}{|{v}\rangle}
where
\phi
is some arbitrary phase. Putting it back into Eq. (
5.37
), we get that
\phi=0
. As a consequence, we obtain the following relation

\displaystyle A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}\,{|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle}={|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle}\qquad(k=1,\ldots,d-1).

(5.40)

Let us now consider the condition (
5.38
) and express
S(\bm{\alpha})
as

S(\bm{\alpha})=S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}-S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}
where
S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}
is a positive matrix spanned by the eigenvectors corresponding to the positive eigenvalues of
S(\bm{\alpha})
and
S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}
is a positive matrix spanned by the eigenvectors corresponding to the negative eigenvalues of
S(\bm{\alpha})
. Plugging this decomposition in (
5.38
), we get that

\displaystyle\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}|\psi\rangle=1+\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}|\psi\rangle

(5.41)

Notice now from Eq. (
5.32
) that the maximum eigenvalue of
S(\bm{\alpha})
is one and as a consequence
\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}|\psi\rangle\leq 1
and also
\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}^{2}|\psi\rangle\leq 1
. Thus, from the above condition (
5.41
), we can conclude that
\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}|\psi\rangle=0
which implies that
S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}|\psi\rangle=0
as
S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}
is positive. As a consequence, we also have that
\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}|\psi\rangle=1
. Going back to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
5.39
), we substitute
{|{u}\rangle}={|{\psi}\rangle}
and
{|{v}\rangle}=S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle
. Let us now compute
|\langle{v}{|{v}\rangle}|
by using the decomposition of
S(\bm{\alpha})
and also using the fact that it is hermitian

\displaystyle\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})|\psi\rangle\leq\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})^{2}|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\psi|\left(S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}^{2}+S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}^{2}+S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}+S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}\right)|\psi\rangle

(5.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\psi|S(\bm{\alpha})_{+}^{2}|\psi\rangle\leq 1

where to get to the second line of the above expression we used that
S(\bm{\alpha})_{-}|\psi\rangle=0
. Now, as concluded before, we get that
{|{u}\rangle}=e^{i\phi}{|{v}\rangle}
where
\phi
is some arbitrary phase. Now, again using Eq. (
5.38
) thus we finally arrive at the relation

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left[\gamma(\bm{\alpha})A_{1}^{k}\otimes B_{k|1}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})A_{0}^{k}\right]\right){|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle}={|{\psi_{AB}}\rangle}.

(5.43)

The relations (
5.40
) and (
5.43
) would be particularly useful for certification of the quantum states and measurements that achieve the maximal violation of the steering functional
I_{2,d,2}
. Let us now show that observation of maximal violation of our steering inequalities allows us to certify the state shared between Alice and Bob and also the measurements performed by Bob.

5.3
ISDI certification of all pure bipartite entangled states

Here, we present the 1SDI certification of all pure bipartite entangled states using the saturation of the quantum bound of the steering functional (
5.1
)
I_{2,d,2}=\beta_{Q}
. Let us first recall that we can only characterise Bob’s observables on the support of his local state
\rho_{B}
. Thus, without loss of generality we assume it to be full rank. This can also be understood as
that Bob’s observables and local state
\rho_{B}
act on the same Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Consider that Alice and Bob perform the quantum steering experiment and observe that the steering functional

\displaystyle I_{2,d,2}(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left\langle A_{0}^{k}\otimes B_{k|0}+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})A_{1}^{k}\otimes B_{k|1}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})A_{0}^{k}\right\rangle,

(5.44)

attains the maximal quantum value
\beta_{Q}=d
where
d
denotes the number of outcomes of each measurement. Alice is trusted and her measurements are given by
A_{0}=Z_{d}
and
A_{1}=X_{d}
[cf. Eq. (
2.91
)]. Let us say that the maximal quantum bound is achieved using the state
\rho_{AB}
acting on
\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
and Bob’s generalised observables
B_{i}\ (i\in\{1,2\})
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
. Then, the following statements hold true for any integer
d\geq 2
:

1.

Bob’s measurements are projective. Equaivalently, the operators
B_{k|i}
for all
k,i
are unitary and
B_{k|i}=B_{1|i}^{k}\equiv B_{i}^{k}
.

2.

Bob’s Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{B}
admits a decomposition into a
d-
dimensional Hilbert space
\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{B^{\prime}}
and some unknown but finite dimensional auxiliary Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{B}=(\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{B^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}.

(5.45)

3.

There exists a local unitary on Bob’s side
U_{B}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes U_{B})\rho_{AB}(\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes U_{B}^{\dagger})={|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}\!{\langle{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}|}_{AB^{\prime}}\otimes\rho_{B^{\prime\prime}}^{aux}.

(5.46)

where
{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}
is the state given in (
5.35
) and

\displaystyle\forall i,\quad U_{B}\,B_{i}\,U_{B}^{\dagger}=A_{i}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}},

(5.47)

where
B^{\prime\prime}
denotes Bob’s auxiliary system.

Proof.

The proof is divided into two major steps. In the first step, we exploit the relations (
5.40
) and (
5.43
) to find Bob’s observables that result in the maximal violation of the steering inequality (
5.1
). Again, using the relations (
5.40
) and (
5.43
) and the derived Bob’s observables, we find the family of states shared beween Alice and Bob parametrised by the collection of numbers
\bm{\alpha}
. For our proof, as was discussed before in Chapter
2
, the state shared between Alice and Bob
\rho_{AB}
is purified by adding an ancillary system
E
possessed by some external agent, named Eve, such that
\rho_{AB}=\mathrm{Tr}_{E}({|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|})
where
{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}\in\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}
.

Bob’s observables

Before finding the explicit forms of Bob’s observables that maximally
violate our steering inequality (
5.1
), we show that these generalised observables must correspond to projective measurements. Let us concentrate on Bob’s first observable and follow the exact same technique as was used in Chapter
4
from Eqs . (
4.38
) to (
4.42
) as the relations (
5.40
) and (
4.38
) are identical. First, we apply

Z_{d}^{d-k}\otimes B_{d-k|0}
to the relation (
5.23
) and then recalling that
Z_{d}
is unitary as well as that
B_{d-k|0}=B_{k|0}^{\dagger}
from (
2.22
), we obtain that

\mathbb{1}_{AE}\otimes(B_{k|0}^{\dagger}B_{k|0}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}={|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.48)

Taking a partial trace over the subsystems
AE
, we arrive at the following condition

(B_{k|0}^{\dagger}B_{k|0})\rho_{B}=\rho_{B},

(5.49)

where
\rho_{B}=\mathrm{Tr}_{AE}[{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|}]
. Recall that
\rho_{B}
is full-rank and thus it is non-singular and invertible. This allows us to immediately conclude that
B_{k|0}^{\dagger}B_{k|0}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
and consequently
B_{k|0}B_{k|0}^{\dagger}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
, and thus
B_{k|0}
is unitary for any
k=0,\ldots,d-1
. Now using Fact
1
, we can conclude that Bob’s measurements are projective, that is, the positive semi-definite operators representing the measurement are mutually orthogonal projectors. Further, the fact that
B_{0}
is projective imposes that
B_{k|0}
are powers of
B_{1|0}
[see Chapter
2
]. As a consequence, from now on we can simply denote
B_{k|0}=B_{0}^{k}
, where
B_{0}\equiv B_{1|0}
.

Let us now focus on Bob’s second observable and show that it corresponds to a projective measurement too.
For this purpose, we refer to the second condition (
5.32
) and then consider the general representation of any state
{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}\in\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}
as in Eq. (
5.25
)

{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}{|{i}\rangle}_{A}{|{e_{i}}\rangle}_{BE},

(5.50)

where
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
and
{|{e_{i}}\rangle}_{BE}
are vectors that are in general not orthogonal. Now recall that, we showed in the previous subsection we showed that any state satisfying the condition (
5.32
) must be locally full-rank. Thus, in the state (
5.50
),
\lambda_{i}>0
for all
i
. For simplicity, from here on we drop all the subscripts in the notation of the state. Now, by plugging this state in the condition
{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|}S(\bm{\alpha}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=1
, we obtain [cf. Eq. (
5.30
)]

\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-k}\mathrm{Re}\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}[B_{k|1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}]{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}\lambda_{j}^{2}.

(5.51)

Dropping the identity acting on Eve,
\mathbb{1}_{E}
, for the time being and then using
the inequality (
5.13
) with in
u_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
v_{i}=\alpha_{i}
, we arrive at

\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-k}\mathrm{Re}\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)\geq\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\right)^{2}.

(5.52)

Notice that the term on the right hand side of the above inequality can be expanded as
\left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\right)^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-k}
. As a consequence, we immediately obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i-k}\mathrm{Re}\left[\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)-1\right]\geq 0.

(5.53)

Again, recalling that
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\leq\mathbb{1}_{B}
for any
k
allows us to conclude that
\mathrm{Re}\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}[B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)\leq 1

for any
i
and
k
. However,
\lambda_{i}
being non-negative in the inequality (
5.52
), forces the term inside the square brackets to be
0
for all
i,k
. Thus, we finally obtain

\mathrm{Re}\left({\langle{e_{i}}|}B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}\right)=1.

(5.54)

As the states
{|{e_{i}}\rangle}
are normalised, the above condition is satisfied iff
B_{k|1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}={|{e_{i}}\rangle}
. For this purpose, we again employ the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
5.39
) where
{|{u}\rangle}={|{e_{i}}\rangle}
and
{|{v}\rangle}=B_{k|1}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}
and then the fact that
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\leq\mathbb{1}
from which we obtain
B_{k|1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}=e^{i\phi}{|{e_{i}}\rangle}
for some arbitrary phase
\phi
. Again using Eq. (
5.54
), we get that
\phi=0
.
Now, we multiply this equation with its conjugate transpose, to observe that

{\langle{e_{i-k}}|}[B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}]{|{e_{i-k}}\rangle}=1.

(5.55)

for any
i,k
. This implies that for any
k
the above condition is satisfied for any
i
. As a consequence, we arrive at a simple relation for any
k

{\langle{e_{i}}|}[B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}]{|{e_{i}}\rangle}=1\qquad(i=0,\ldots,d-1).

(5.56)

Tracing out Eve’s subsystem, further implies that

\mathrm{Tr}[B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\rho_{B}^{i}]=1
for all
i
, where
\rho_{B}^{i}=\mathrm{Tr}_{E}[{|{e_{i}}\rangle}\!{\langle{e_{i}}|}_{BE}]
.
As
\rho_{B}^{i}
is positive and again recalling that
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}\leq\mathbb{1}_{B}
for any
k
allows us to conclude that this condition holds true iff
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}
is an identity acting onto the support of
\rho_{B}^{i}
. However, notice that the support of Bob’s reduced state
\rho_{B}=\mathrm{Tr}_{AE}[{|{\psi}\rangle}\!{\langle{\psi}|}_{ABE}]
is in-fact composed of the supports of
\rho_{B}^{i}
’s. To see this, we use the decomposition of the state
{|{\psi}\rangle}_{ABE}
given in (
5.50
) to obtain that

\displaystyle\rho_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{AE}\left[\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}{|{i}\rangle}\!{\langle{j}|}\otimes{|{e_{i}}\rangle}\!{\langle{e_{j}}|}\right]

(5.57)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{E}\left[\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}^{2}{|{e_{i}}\rangle}\!{\langle{e_{i}}|}\right]=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\lambda_{i}^{2}\rho_{B}^{i}.

As a consequence,
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}
is an identity that acts on the entire support of Bob’s reduced state
\rho_{B}
, and thus we finally have that
B_{k|1}^{\dagger}B_{k|1}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
and consequently
B_{k|1}B_{k|1}^{\dagger}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
for all
k
. Again, using Fact
1
, we can conclude that the second Bob’s observable corresponds to projective measurements and hence from here on, we denote
B_{k|1}=B_{1}^{k}
, where
B_{1}\equiv B_{1|1}
.
This completes the part of the proof to show that the maximal violation of the steering inequality (
5.1
) can only be achieved when Bob’s both measurements are projective.

Now, we move onto finding the explicit form of Bob’s both observables. Let us first consider the relation (
5.43
) and then apply

\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes B_{1}
to it, which after rearranging some terms gives us

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(X_{d}^{k}\otimes B_{1}^{k+1}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\left[\left(\mathbb{1}_{A}-\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{k}\right)\otimes B_{1}\right]{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.58)

To simplify the notation, let us introduce the following operator

\overline{Z}_{A}:=\mathbb{1}_{A}-\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{k}.

(5.59)

Now, an application of
Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}
to the left hand side of Eq. (
5.58
) gives us

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(Z_{d}^{-1}X_{d}^{k}\otimes B_{1}^{k+1}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(\overline{Z}_{A}Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes B_{1}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle},

(5.60)

where we can interchange the positioning of
Z_{d}
and
\overline{Z}_{A}
as they commute. Then, by using the commutation relation
Z_{d}X_{d}=\omega X_{d}Z_{d}
, we can rewrite Eq. (
5.60
) as

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\omega^{-k}X_{d}^{k}\otimes B_{1}^{k+1}\right)\left(Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes B_{1})\left(Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle},

(5.61)

where we again used the fact that
Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}
and
\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes B_{1}
commute. Now, using Eq. (
5.40
) for
k=1
, that is,
\left(Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes B_{0}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}
, we finally get

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\omega^{-k}X_{d}^{k}\otimes B_{1}^{k+1}B_{0}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes B_{1}B_{0}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.62)

Applying
B_{0}
from the left hand side of the expression (
5.58
), we obtain

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(X_{d}^{k}\otimes B_{0}B_{1}^{k+1}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes B_{0}B_{1}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.63)

In the next step, we multiply
\omega^{-1}
to Eq. (
5.62
) and then subtract it from Eq.
(
5.63
)
, which immediately gives us

\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left[X_{d}^{k}\otimes\left(B_{0}B_{1}^{k+1}-\omega^{-(k+1)}B_{1}^{k+1}B_{0}\right)\right]{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=[\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes(B_{0}B_{1}-\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0})]{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.64)

Let us again consider the relation (
5.43
) and multiply it by
X_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}
from the left hand side, which gives us

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\gamma(\bm{\alpha})X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes B_{1}^{k}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(X_{d}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.65)

Then, after multiplying
\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes B_{0}
to the above equation and then taking into account that it commutes with
X_{d}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}
, it not difficult to see that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\gamma(\bm{\alpha})X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes B_{0}B_{1}^{k}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(X_{d}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes B_{0}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.66)

Now, let us exploit the relation (
5.40
) for
k=1
, that is,
\left(Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes B_{0}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}
and then using the fact that
\overline{Z}_{A}
and
Z_{d}
commutes, we finally get,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\gamma(\bm{\alpha})X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes B_{0}B_{1}^{k}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(X_{d}^{-1}Z_{d}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.67)

Next, we apply
Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}
to Eq. (
5.65
) from the left hand side to obtain,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\gamma(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{-1}X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes B_{1}^{k}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(Z_{d}^{-1}X_{d}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.68)

Again, by employing the relation
Z_{d}X_{d}=\omega X_{d}Z_{d}
, the above equation (
5.68
) can be rewriten as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left(\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\omega^{-(k-1)}X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes B_{1}^{k}B_{0}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\left(\omega X_{d}^{-1}Z_{d}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.69)

Notice that in the left hand side of the above equation, we again exploited the relation (
5.40
) for
k=1
, that is,
\left(Z_{d}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes B_{0}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}
. Now we apply
\omega^{-1}
to the above equation (
5.69
) and then subtract it from Eq. (
5.66
) to get

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left[X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes\left(B_{0}B_{1}^{k}-\omega^{-k}B_{1}^{k}B_{0}\right)\right]{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=0,

(5.70)

which can be divided up into two parts by separating the term corresponding to
k=1
, as follows

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=2}^{d-1}\left[X_{d}^{k-1}\otimes\left(B_{0}B_{1}^{k}-\omega^{-k}B_{1}^{k}B_{0}\right)\right]{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=-\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(B_{0}B_{1}-\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.71)

Notice that the left hand side of the expressions (
5.64
) and (
5.71
) are identical, which immediately allows us to get that

\displaystyle\overline{Z}_{A}\otimes(B_{0}B_{1}-\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=-\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\left(B_{0}B_{1}-\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle},

(5.72)

which after a simple rearrangement of the terms and expanding
\overline{Z}_{A}
from (
5.59
) yields,

\displaystyle\left[(1+\gamma(\bm{\alpha}))\mathbb{1}_{A}-\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{k}\right]\otimes\left(B_{0}B_{1}-\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0}\right){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=0.

(5.73)

As proven in Observation
5.1
stated in Appendix
C
, the operator
[1+\gamma(\bm{\alpha})]\mathbb{1}-\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{k}
is invertible. Thus, taking trace over the subsystems
A,E
allows us to finally conclude that

(B_{0}B_{1}-\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0})\rho_{B}=0,

(5.74)

where
\rho_{B}=\mathrm{Tr}_{AE}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|}
. Recalling that
\rho_{B}
is full-rank and thus invertible, the above expression (
5.74
) implies the following
commutation relation between Bob’s both observables

B_{0}B_{1}=\omega^{-1}B_{1}B_{0}.

(5.75)

As stated in Fact
2
which was proven in Ref.
[
46
]
, the above relation along with the fact that
B_{0}^{d}=B_{1}^{d}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
imposes that Bob’s Hilbert space decomposes into a tensor product
\mathcal{H}_{B}=(\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{B^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}

where
\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
is some Hilbert space of unknown but finite dimension. Along with it, there also exists a unitary transformation

U_{B}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that

\displaystyle U_{B}\,B_{0}\,U^{\dagger}_{B}=Z^{*}_{d}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}},\quad U_{B}\,B_{1}\,U_{B}^{\dagger}=X_{d}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}},

(5.76)

where
\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
is the identity acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
. This completes the characterisation of Bob’s observables that maximally violate the steering inequality (
5.1
).

The state

We finally have all the tools required to find the state that maximally violates the steering inequality (
5.1
). As was derived in the previous part, up to a local unitary Bob’s both observables are the ideal ones (
5.76
). Thus, we can rewrite the relation (
5.23
) and (
5.32
) by plugging in Bob’s derived observables as

(Z_{d}\otimes Z_{d}^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}E}){|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}={|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle},

(5.77)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}\left[\gamma(\bm{\alpha})X_{d}^{k}\otimes X_{d}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}E}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{BE}\right]{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}={|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle},

(5.78)

where
{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}=U_{B}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{AE}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}
. From here on, for convenience we drop the all the identities from the above relations. As concluded in the previous part of the proof that Bob’s Hilbert space is of dimension
(\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{B^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
due to which the state
{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}
belongs to
(\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{A}\otimes(\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{B^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}
. As a consequence, any such state can be written using the computational basis in
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
as,

{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}{|{i}\rangle}_{A}{|{j}\rangle}_{B^{\prime}}{|{\psi_{ij}}\rangle}_{B^{\prime\prime}E},

(5.79)

where
{|{\psi_{ij}}\rangle}_{B^{\prime\prime}E}
is some unnormalised state belonging to
\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E}
.
After plugging this state to the condition (
5.77
) for
k=1
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\omega^{i-j}{|{ij}\rangle}{|{\psi_{ij}}\rangle}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}{|{ij}\rangle}{|{\psi_{ij}}\rangle},

(5.80)

which holds true if and only if

{|{\psi_{ij}}\rangle}=0
for any
i\neq j
. As a consequence, the only terms in the state (
5.79
) remains when
i=j
, and thus we have the simplified form of the state given by

{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}{|{ii}\rangle}{|{\psi_{ii}}\rangle}.

(5.81)

Let us now consider the condition (
5.78
) where we can extend the range of the sum to
k=0
by recalling that the
0-th
power of an observable is identity and also that
\delta_{0}(\bm{\alpha})=-1
. Thus, after some rearrangement of the terms we get the following expression

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\left[\gamma(\bm{\alpha})X_{d}^{k}\otimes X_{d}^{k}+\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha})Z_{d}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B}\right]{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}=\gamma(\bm{\alpha}){|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}.

(5.82)

Plugging in the simplified form of
{|{\tilde{\psi}_{ABE}}\rangle}
as derived in (
5.81
), the above expression turns out to be

\displaystyle\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}{|{i+k}\rangle}{|{i+k}\rangle}{|{\psi_{ii}}\rangle}+\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\omega^{ki}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha}){|{ii}\rangle}{|{\psi_{ii}}\rangle}=\gamma(\bm{\alpha})\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}{|{ii}\rangle}{|{\psi_{ii}}\rangle}.

(5.83)

Mulitplying the above expression with
{\langle{ss}|}
from the lest hand side, we obtain that

\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\gamma(\bm{\alpha}){|{\psi_{s\ominus k,s\ominus k}}\rangle}+\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\omega^{ks}\delta_{k}(\bm{\alpha}){|{\psi_{ss}}\rangle}=\gamma(\bm{\alpha}){|{\psi_{ss}}\rangle}

(5.84)

where
s\ominus k
represents
s-k
modulo
d
. We can simplify the above expression by substituting the explicit form of
\delta(\bm{\alpha})
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}{|{\psi_{s\ominus k,s\ominus k}}\rangle}-\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\frac{\omega^{k(d-j+s)}}{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=0\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{d-1}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{j}}{|{\psi_{ss}}\rangle}={|{\psi_{ss}}\rangle}

(5.85)

Now, using the identity
\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\omega^{k(j-s)}=d\delta_{j,s}
, we can simplify the above expression to find the explicit form of the state
{|{\psi_{ss}}\rangle}
given by

{|{\psi_{ss}}\rangle}=\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{d-1}}{|{\Psi}\rangle},

(5.86)

where we denoted
{|{\Psi}\rangle}=\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}{|{\psi_{s\ominus k,s\ominus k}}\rangle}\equiv\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}{|{\psi_{kk}}\rangle}
for any
s
. As a consequence, Eq. (
5.81
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(U_{B}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{AE}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{m=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}{|{ii}\rangle}_{AB^{\prime}}\right)\otimes{|{\xi}\rangle}_{B^{\prime\prime}E}={|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}_{AB^{\prime}}\otimes{|{\xi}\rangle}_{B^{\prime\prime}E},

(5.87)

where

{|{\xi}\rangle}_{B^{\prime\prime}E}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}+\ldots+\alpha_{d-1}}{|{\Psi}\rangle}.

(5.88)

This finally completes the proof of certification of all pure bipartite entangled states along with a pair of arbitrary outcome mutually unbiased bases in the 1SDI scenario.
∎

Let us now proceed towards another important result of this chapter that utilises the above theorem (
5.2
) involving certification of every rank-one extremal POVM’s.

5.4
Certification of all rank-one extremal POVM

As discussed before in Chapter
2
, let us consider a rank-one extremal measurement denoted as
\mathcal{I}=\{\mathcal{I}_{b}\}
, such that
b
represents its outcomes and
\mathcal{I}_{b}
represents the measurement operator corresponding to the
b-th
outcome. These measurement operators are positive semi-definite and sum up to one. Additionally, it was shown in
[
80
]
that measurement operators of an extremal rank-one POVM are projectors scaled down by some non-negative real number, that is,
\mathcal{I}_{b}=\lambda_{b}{|{\mu}\rangle}\!{\langle{\mu}|}
with
{|{\mu}\rangle}\in\mathbbm{C}^{d}
and
0\leq\lambda_{b}\leq 1
.

Figure 5.1
:
1SDI scenario to certify any rank-one extremal POVM: Alice and Bob receive subsystems from the preparation device on which they perform
d+1
and
3
measurements respectively such that Alice is trusted. All the measurements are
d-
outcome except Bob’s third measurement which is of
d^{2}-
outcome.

It turns our that the observation of the maximal violation of our steering inequality (
5.1
) plus an additional set of conditions enables us to design a simple method that can be used for certification of any extremal rank-one POVM. For this purpose, we again consider the 1SDI setting such that Alice is again trusted but now performs
d+1
measurements corresponding to the observables
A_{0}=Z_{d}
and
A_{i+1}=X_{d}Z_{d}^{i}
for
i=0,1,\ldots,d-1
. Bob is untrusted and performs three measurements, where the first two measurements are
d-
outcome and the third one has
d^{2}-
outcomes. The scenario is depicted in Fig.
5.1
.

Notice that the statistics corresponding to both Alice and Bob choosing the input
0,1
, allow us to employ the steering inequality (
5.1
) and certify any pure bipartite entangled state using Theorem
5.2
. Without loss of generality, Bob’s third measurement, which is a
d^{2}-
outcome POVM, is denoted as
\{R_{b}\}
. Further, notice that the operators
X_{d}Z_{d}^{i}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,d-1
are not proper observables based on the definition introduced in Chapter
2
as
(X_{d}Z_{d}^{i})^{d}=\omega^{id(d-1)}\mathbb{1}
. Thus, dividing the matrices with the scalar
\omega^{-i(d-1)}
yields proper observables, but for simplicity we would drop this factor from further considerations. It is also worth noting that the statistics obtained from the
d+1
observables,
Z_{d}
and
X_{d}Z_{d}^{i}
, are enough to simulate the statistics corresponding to the operators
X_{d}^{i}Z_{d}^{j}
for
i,j=0,1,\ldots,d-1
that represent the Heisenberg-Weyl (HW) basis
[
178
]

2
2

2

The HW basis is a collection of operators that forms a basis for operators that act on
d-
dimensional Hilbert space with
d
being any positive integer.
. The reason for this fact is that every element in the HW basis can be generated by considering the powers of
Z_{d}
and
X_{d}Z_{d}^{i}
for all
i
and then multiply them with appropriate powers of
\omega
. For instance,
X_{d}^{2}Z_{d}^{2}
can be generated by taking
X_{d}Z_{d}
two times and then multiplying it with
\omega^{-1}
, that is,
X_{d}^{2}Z_{d}^{2}=\omega^{-1}(X_{d}Z_{d})^{2}
. The result is stated below as a simple theorem.

Theorem 5.3
.

Assume that Alice and Bob perform the quantum steering experiment and are able to certify that the state shared among them as well as the measurements along with the Hilbert space of Bob as given in Theorem-
5.2
. Consider then a POVM
R=\{R_{b}\}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B}=(\mathbbm{C}^{d})_{B^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
. If for some extremal
POVM
\mathcal{I}=\{\mathcal{I}_{b}\}
acting on
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
the following identities

\displaystyle\langle X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes R_{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}\rangle_{{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}}=\langle X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{b}\rangle_{{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}}

(5.89)

hold true for any
i,j=0,\ldots,d-1
, where
{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=(\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes U_{B}^{\dagger}){|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}_{AB^{\prime}}\otimes{|{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}\rangle}
from (
5.46
) where
{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}_{AB^{\prime}}
is the ideal state defined in Eq. (
5.35
). Then, there exist a unitary transformation
U_{B}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that the measurement operators of the POVM
R
are equivalent to the measurement operators of the ideal POVM
\mathcal{I}
as

\displaystyle U_{B}\ R_{b}\ U_{B}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{I}_{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\qquad\forall b.

(5.90)

Proof.

Our proof takes inspiration from the technique introduced in Ref.
[
54
]
, where extremal POVM’s acting on two-dimensional Hilbert space were certified up to certain equivalences. Here, we generalise that approach to POVM’s that act on arbitrary dimensional Hilbert space in the scenario where Alice is trusted. Let us first observe that the statistics one observes from the actual experiment must be equivalent to one observed in the ideal experiment, that is,

\displaystyle\forall\ b\quad\forall i,j\qquad{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes R_{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}={\langle{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{b}{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}.

(5.91)

Solving the above condition is enough to certify the POVM
R
. For this purpose, as was done in Chapter
4
, we first rewrite the state
{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}
in
terms of the maximally entangled state of two qudits
{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}
[see Eq. (
4.16
)] as

{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}=[\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes P(\bm{\alpha})]{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle},

(5.92)

where

P(\bm{\alpha})=\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{i}{|{i}\rangle}\!{\langle{i}|}.

(5.93)

Recall that
\alpha_{i}>0
for all
i
and
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{2}=1
. Let us also introduce another set of
d^{2}
number of operators, derived from HW basis

W_{i,j}:=P(\bm{\alpha})^{-1}\left(X^{i}Z^{j}\right)^{*}P(\bm{\alpha})^{-1}.

(5.94)

Let us observe that the above operators are linearly independent as
P(\bm{\alpha})
is invertible and
X^{i}Z^{j}
are orthogonal in the Hilbert-Schmidt scalar product, that is,
\mathrm{Tr}[X^{i}Z^{j}(X^{i^{\prime}}Z^{j^{\prime}})^{\dagger}]=d\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{j,j^{\prime}}
. As a consequence, the set
\{W_{i,j}\}
forms a complete basis for operators acting on
d-
dimensional Hilbert space. Recalling that the measurement operators
\mathcal{I}_{b}
of the ideal POVM act on
d-
dimensional Hilbert space and thus, using the newly defined operator basis (
5.94
), we can express them as

\mathcal{I}_{b}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}l^{b}_{i,j}W_{i,j}\qquad\forall b,

(5.95)

where
l_{i,j}^{b}
are in general complex coefficients. Let us now compute the right hand side of the expression (
5.91
) by plugging in the above representation of the POVM
\mathcal{I}
,

\displaystyle{\langle{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{b}{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}l^{b}_{m,n}\!{\langle{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes P(\bm{\alpha})^{-1}\left(X^{m}Z^{n}\right)^{*}P(\bm{\alpha})^{-1}{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}

(5.96)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}l^{b}_{m,n}\!{\langle{\phi_{+}^{d}}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes\left(X^{m}Z^{n}\right)^{*}{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle},

where we exploited the form of the state
{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}
given in (
5.92
). Now, exploiting the identity
(R\otimes Q)\,{|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}=(RQ^{T}\otimes\mathbb{1}){|{\phi_{+}^{d}}\rangle}
that is satisfied for any two matrices
Q
and
R
acting on
d-
dimensional Hilbert space [see Fact
5
in Appendix
A
] and also the fact that
X^{i}Z^{j}
form an orthogonal basis as mentioned above, we finally obtain that

{\langle{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{b}{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}=l^{b}_{i,j}\qquad\forall b.

(5.97)

Next, our aim is to compute the left hand side of the expression (
5.91
). We use the fact that according to Theorem
5.2
, Bob’s Hilbert space decomposes as
\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathbbm{C}^{d}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
and there exist a unitary
U_{B}:\mathcal{H}_{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{B}
that transforms that transforms the state
{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}
as

\displaystyle(\mathbb{1}_{AE}\otimes U_{B}){|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}={|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}_{AB^{\prime}}\otimes{|{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}\rangle}.

(5.98)

Now, any measurement operator of the POVM
R
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
can be expressed using the basis (
5.94
) as,

\displaystyle U_{B}R_{b}U_{B}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}W_{i,j}\otimes\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b},

(5.99)

where
\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}
are general operators acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
.
Now, computing the left hand side of (
5.91
) by plugging in it the above mentioned form of
R_{b}
(
5.99
) and the state
{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}
, we have

{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes R_{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}=\left[\sum_{m,n}\!{\langle{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes W_{m,n}{|{\psi(\bm{\alpha})}\rangle}\right]{\langle{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}\rangle}.

(5.100)

As was computed above to get (
5.97
) from (
5.96
), the term inside the square bracket in the above expression is just
1
. Thus, we finally arrive at

{\langle{\psi_{ABE}}|}X^{i}Z^{j}\otimes R_{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{\psi_{ABE}}\rangle}={\langle{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{E}{|{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}\rangle}=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}\sigma_{B^{\prime\prime}}\right)\qquad\forall b,

(5.101)

where
\sigma_{B^{\prime\prime}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{E}({|{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\xi_{B^{\prime\prime}E}}|})
. Let us now decompose
\sigma_{B^{\prime\prime}}
using its eigenvectors denoted by
{|{k}\rangle}
as
\sigma_{B^{\prime\prime}}=\sum_{k}p_{k}{|{k}\rangle}\!{\langle{k}|}
. Plugging this into the above expression, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}\sigma_{B^{\prime\prime}}\right)=\sum_{k}p_{k}{\langle{k}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}{|{k}\rangle}.

(5.102)

Recalling again the identity (
5.91
) and then using Eq. (
5.97
), we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{k}p_{k}{\langle{k}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}{|{k}\rangle}=l_{i,j}^{b}.

(5.103)

Next, we introduce a family of POVM’s
k
as,
\mathcal{I}_{k}=\{\mathcal{I}_{b,k}\}
, whose measurement operators are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{b,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[\left(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes{|{k}\rangle}\!{\langle{k}|}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\right)R_{b}\right]

(5.104)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\!{\langle{k}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}{|{k}\rangle}W_{i,j}.

As
R
is a valid POVM, as discussed in Chapter
2
all its measurement operators are hermitian and positive semi-definite, that is,
R_{b}\geq 0
for all
b
. Consequently, from the first line of Eq. (
5.104
), we can see that
\mathcal{I}_{b,k}
is also positive semi-definite, that is,
\mathcal{I}_{b,k}\geq 0
for any
k
and
b
, as product of two positive semi-definite matrices is also positive semi-definite. Further,
\sum_{b}R_{b}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
is identity and then using the first line of of Eq. (
5.104
), we can directly see that
\sum_{b}\mathcal{I}_{b,k}=\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}
for any
k
. As a consequence, the family of POVM’s
\{\mathcal{I}_{b}^{k}\}_{b}
are valid quantum measurements.
Let us now go back to Eq. (
5.103
), and then rewrite it using the family of POVM’s (
5.104
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{b}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}l_{i,j}^{b}W_{i,j}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{d-1}\sum_{k}p_{k}{\langle{k}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}{|{k}\rangle}W_{i,j}=\sum_{k}p_{k}\mathcal{I}_{b,k}.

(5.105)

However, the POVM
\mathcal{I}
is extremal and can not be decomposed as a convex mixture of other POVM’s. Thus, we can immediately conclude that

\displaystyle\forall k\qquad\mathcal{I}_{b,k}=\mathcal{I}_{b},

(5.106)

which is equivalent to the condition,

\displaystyle\forall k\qquad{\langle{k}|}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b}{|{k}\rangle}=l_{i,j}^{b}.

(5.107)

Next, we consider the following vectors belonging to
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
:

\displaystyle{|{\varphi_{a,s,t}}\rangle}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left({|{s}\rangle}\pm\mathbbm{i}^{a}{|{t}\rangle}\right),

(5.108)

where
a=0,1
and
{|{s}\rangle}
and
{|{t}\rangle}
are two distinct vectors belonging to the eigenbasis
\{{|{k}\rangle}\}
of
\sigma_{B^{\prime\prime}}
. We now compute the following quantity

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[\left(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes|\varphi_{a,s,t}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{a,s,t}|_{B^{\prime\prime}}\right)R_{b}\right]=\sum_{i,j}\mathrm{Tr}(|\varphi_{a,s,t}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{a,s,t}|_{B^{\prime\prime}}\widetilde{R}_{i,j}^{b})W_{i,j}.

(5.109)

Expanding the above quantity using the explicit form of the vectors given in (
5.108
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[\left(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes|\varphi_{a,s,t}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{a,s,t}|_{B^{\prime\prime}}\right)R_{b}\right]=\mathcal{I}_{b}\pm\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes L^{a}_{B^{\prime\prime}})R_{b}\right],

(5.110)

where

\displaystyle L_{B^{\prime\prime}}^{a}=(\mathbbm{i}^{a}/2)\left(|t\rangle\!\langle s|+(-1)^{a}|s\rangle\!\langle t|\right).

(5.111)

The fact that
R_{b}\geq 0
, imposes that left-hand side of the above
expression is non-negative as it is a product of two matrices that are positive semi-definite matrices. This allows us to conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{b}\geq\pm\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes L^{a}_{B^{\prime\prime}})R_{b}\right].

(5.112)

As discussed above, the measurement operators of any rank-one extremal POVM acting on
d-
dimensional Hilbert space can be expressed as
\mathcal{I}_{b}=\lambda_{b}{|{\mu_{b}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\mu_{b}}|}
, where the vectors
{|{\mu_{b}}\rangle}
are normalized and belong to
\mathbbm{C}^{d}
. Using this fact, we show in Observation
5.2
stated in Appendix
C
that the operator appearing on the right-hand side of the above expression (
5.112
) must be rank one as well and must admit the following form

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes L^{a}_{B^{\prime\prime}})R_{b}\right]=\lambda^{\prime}_{b}{|{\mu_{b}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\mu_{b}}|},

(5.113)

such that
\lambda_{b}\geq\pm\lambda^{\prime}_{b}
. Recalling that
\sum_{b}R_{b}=\mathbb{1}_{B}
and that

\mathrm{Tr}L_{B^{\prime\prime}}^{a}=0
for any
a
, we can finally conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{b}\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes L^{a}_{B^{\prime\prime}})R_{b}\right]=0=\sum_{b}\lambda_{b}^{\prime}{|{\mu_{b}}\rangle}\!{\langle{\mu_{b}}|}.

(5.114)

Since
\mathcal{I}_{b}
are linearly independent, we learn from the above condition that
\lambda^{\prime}_{b}=0
for all
b
which in turn implies from (
5.113
) that
\mathrm{Tr}_{B^{\prime\prime}}\left[(\mathbb{1}_{B^{\prime}}\otimes L^{a}_{B^{\prime\prime}})R_{b}\right]=0
for any
b
and
a
. Finally expanding the left hand side of (
5.113
) by plugging in the explicit form of
L^{a}_{B^{\prime\prime}}
and also taking into account that
X^{i}Z^{j}
are linearly independent for any
i,j
gives us two simple conditions:
